Multi-model forecasting of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} in Abu Dhabi: benefits of correlation-based feature augmentation
Authors: Waad Abuouelezz, Nazar Ali, Zeyar Aung, Ahmed Altunaiji, Shaik Basheeruddin Shah, Mansour Alkatheeri
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Air pollution, particularly Nitrogen Dioxide (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_2$$\end{document}) and ground-level ozone (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_3$$\end{document}), poses major risks to human health and the environment, making accurate forecasting essential for effective public health management. This paper compares several machine learning and deep learning models including Decision Trees, Random Forests, Support Vector Regression, Convolutional Neural Networks, Long Short-Term Memory networks, Prophet, and Transformers, for prediction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_2$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_3$$\end{document} levels in Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates. Model performance is evaluated using symmetric Mean Absolute Percentage Error (sMAPE), Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE), and Mean Absolute Error (MAE) across forecasting horizons of 1 hour, 2 hours, 1 day, and 1 week. We find that Transformers yielded best results, with average sMAPE values ranging from 0.2354–0.2981 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_2$$\end{document} and 0.2030–0.2719 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_3$$\end{document}. Convolutional Neural Network is the second-best performer for short- and medium-forecasts, while Prophet is second-best for longer horizons. We also observe improved prediction accuracy when the inter-pollutant correlations between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_2$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_3$$\end{document} are considered, particularly for 1-hour \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_2$$\end{document} and 1- to 24-hour \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_3$$\end{document} forecasts. These findings highlight the superiority of Transformer models and the benefits of incorporating pollutant coexistence effects in air quality forecasting.

Introduction

Nitrogen Dioxide (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document}) and ground-level Ozone (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}) are recognized worldwide as among the most harmful pollutants to human health and the environment1. In addition to their adverse effects on human health, they are key components in the formation of secondary pollutants2. In particular, ground-level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}, produced by photochemical reactions between nitrogen oxides (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{x}$$\end{document}) and volatile organic compounds (VOCs), contributes substantially to the formation of smog, acid rain and fine particulate matter (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{2.5}$$\end{document})3. Elevated concentrations of these pollutants degrade air quality, exacerbate respiratory diseases, and worsen overall health. In the United Arab Emirates (UAE), rapid urbanization, population growth, and heavy traffic activity have led to high levels of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}1. Abu Dhabi is particularly affected because of its predominately desert terrain and limited rainfall, which reduces pollutant dispersion and atmospheric cleansing. Monitoring and predicting these pollutants are therefore essential for managing the environment and protecting public health. While ground monitoring stations give valuable information, forecasting models remain necessity to anticipate pollution events and support timely interventions.

The field of air pollutants forecasting has experienced a paradigm shift in modeling approaches, from classical mechanistic models toward sophisticated data-driven methods. Traditionally, knowledge-driven, deterministic, or cause-and-effect relationship models were used for simulating the complex formation, transformation, diffusion, and transport of atmospheric pollutants4,5. Interesting applications of such models are the Urban Airshed Model (UAM) and the Goddard Earth Observing System Composition Forecast (GEOS-CF) product5,6. These models are based on mathematical formulations that describe atmospheric phenomena and provide insight into pollution dynamics. However, they are computationally intensive and highly complex, requiring considerable amount of data such as meteorological information, pollutant emission databases, and detailed knowledge of pollution mechanisms4,6. The performance of these models is further constrained by uncertainties and biases in emission databases and the parameterization of atmospheric processes. For instance, the Integrated Empirical Rate (IER) photochemistry model simplified chemical simulation by expressing the production of smog as a cumulative function of sunlight exposure and ambient air quality such that dispersion and chemical computations can be carried out separately, especially for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{x}$$\end{document}plumes interacting in urban environment4. Despite these simplifications, such mechanistic models often struggle with scalability and real-time forecasting due to their high computational cost and sensitivity to input inaccuracies.

In contrast, statistical and machine learning models can identify non-linear correlations among predictors and pollutant concentrations without requiring prior knowledge of the underlying physical or chemical processes6. These models generally offer greater computational efficiency, rely on fewer types of input variables, and often deliver superior accuracy compared with Chemical Transport Models (CTMs). Among the earliest data-driven approaches are the AutoRegressive Integrated Moving Average (ARIMA) and Seasonal ARIMA (SARIMA), both of which have been extensively used for long-term air pollutant forecasting7. Although these models excel at capturing linear associations and seasonal fluctuations, they commonly fail to represent higher-order non-linear dynamics, and therefore unsuitable for detecting peaks or extreme pollution events. Multiple Linear Regression (MLR) has also been widely used because of its simplicity and low data requirements. However, its application is constrained by its linear structure5,8. Previous research has indicated that while MLR can produce good fits, especially when considering preceding-hour \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} concentrations and synoptic-meteorological variables, it performs poorly for extreme values and non-linear behavior, thereby limiting its ability to generalize across complex atmospheric dynamics.

The growth of computational power has facilitated the advancement of ML algorithms7,9–11. Multilayer Perceptrons (MLPs) or Artificial Neural Networks (ANNs) are well suited for characterizing highly non-linear associations among air pollutant concentrations, while recurrent neural networks, such as Elman neural network, can identify temporal patterns through feedback connections5,12–14. MLP models have been used for predicting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document}concentrations up to 8 hours ahead5. However, ANNs typically require large datasets, long training times, and may overfit without proper regularization. Support Vector Machines (SVMs), Support Vector Regression (SVR), and Kernel Extreme Learning Machines (KELM) have demonstrated superior performance over linear approaches for estimating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document}levels, with further improvements possible when combined with techniques such as wavelet transformation (WT) and partial least squares (PLS)12,15. Despite this, SVM/SVR performance is highly dependent on kernel choice and parameter tuning, which can limit their interpretability and reduce computational efficiency for very large datasets. Random Forest models have also shown promising potential for characterizing spatial variations in air pollutants and have been used for day-to-day \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document}forecasting, frequently surpassing CTMs when acting as statistical correction schemes6. Nevertheless, ensemble models like Random Forests may struggle to capture long-term temporal dependencies because they do not explicitly model sequential structure.

Deep learning models, the current state of the art, are capable of automatically extracting high-level features and processing raw data16. Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) and Bidirectional LSTM (BiLSTM) networks and their variants are especially efficient at learning sequential patterns and temporal dynamics in pollutant time series data7. Recent advances have further demonstrated the effectiveness of hybrid recurrent architectures; for instance, novel hybrid RNN-Bidirectional GRU models have been proposed for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{2.5}$$\end{document}forecasting with random imputation strategies to handle missing observations17, while BiGRU-1DCNN hybrids have been applied for spatio-temporal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{2.5}$$\end{document}forecasting across multiple monitoring stations18. Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) extract spatial features and are frequently integrated with RNNs in ConvLSTM frameworks to handle spatiotemporal dependencies, while Residual Networks (ResNet) assist the extraction of deep abstract spatial interdependencies9,19,20. Hybrid architectures combining 1DCNN with BiLSTM and gradient boosting methods such as XGBoost have also shown strong performance for urban air pollution prediction21, and decomposed-recomposed 1DCNN-BiGRU frameworks have demonstrated further gains by explicitly modeling signal components prior to forecasting22. Yet these hybrid architectures can be difficult to optimize and are prone to high parameterization.

Ensemble and gradient boosting approaches have also gained traction; hierarchical ensemble methods combining LightGBM with residual neural networks have been proposed for multi-country \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{10}$$\end{document}forecasting23, and hybrid stacked deep learning frameworks with explainability modules have been developed for urban \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{10}$$\end{document}concentration prediction24. Data-driven hybrid approaches such as PR-FCNN have further advanced \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{2.5}$$\end{document}concentration prediction by integrating physically informed priors with convolutional feature extraction25. Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) operate on graph-structured data by incorporating topological information of monitoring networks, while hybrid designs such as Res-GCN-BiLSTM combine spatiotemporal, topological, and exogenous variables to improve prediction19. Transformer models, which employ multi-head self-attention mechanisms, have shown exceptional precision in time series forecasting tasks, particularly for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document}, because they can capture complex interactions among near-surface concentrations, satellite retrievals, and meteorological variables without relying on predefined physical relationships9,16. Transformer-based frameworks augmented with country-level embeddings and explainable AI modules have demonstrated both high accuracy and interpretability for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{2.5}$$\end{document}forecasting, offering valuable insights for environmental decision-making26. Global-scale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{2.5}$$\end{document}exposure forecasting using novel deep learning architectures combined with explainable artificial intelligence has further extended the applicability of such models to diverse geographic contexts27. Beyond particulate matter, neural network approaches have also been extended to greenhouse gas prediction, including carbon emission forecasting in industrial and power sectors28.

Transformer variants, such as the stmtTransformer, combine Transformers with Multi-Task Learning (MTL) to simultaneously predict multiple pollutants and exhibit great generalization potential. CNN-Transformer hybrids extract multivariate features and attention mechanisms to deliver superior performance over other architectures in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}projection tasks20. Despite these advances, a consistent limitation across studies is the absence of a unified model capable of handling the diverse temporal behaviors of different monitoring stations, making model performance highly dependent on local dynamics and site-specific pollutant variability. The accuracy of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}predictions is significantly improved when inter-pollutant correlations are incorporated19,20. Because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}are chemically related, their concentrations are closely linked and typically exhibit and inverse diurnal relationship8,29. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} is photochemically degraded in the presence of sunlight and reactive organic compounds to form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}, whereas at nighttime or in areas with high NO levels, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}is reduced through titration reactions16,19,29. Emissions from traffic flow are a primary cause of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{x}$$\end{document}, the precursors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}, thereby influencing Ozone concentrations. Studies show that including other pollutants and precursors, such as CO, VOCs, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {SO}_{2}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{2.5}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{10}$$\end{document}, as input variables, improves predictive accuracy19,20.

Some studies have covered \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} prediction in the UAE and have reported promising outcomes. One study estimated \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document}concentrations using statistical and machine learning models, including NAR-NN, SARIMA, LSTM, and ARIMA, showing symmetric Mean Absolute Percentage Error (sMAPE) values of ”very good” (8.64%) and ”acceptable” (42.45%)7. Another comprehensive study in Abu Dhabi analyzed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} temporal behaviors over a two-year period and compared several state-of-the-art deep learning models, including MiniRocket, ResNet, XceptionTime, InceptionTime, and Transformer. The Transformer model outperformed the others, achieving an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document}of 0.98 and an RMSE of 0.0019. Similarly, focused studies on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}in Abu Dhabi used two years of hourly observations at five monitoring stations30. Advanced deep learning schemes, including LSTM, MiniRocket, InceptionTime, and Transformers, were assessed for one-month-ahead \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} prediction. Transformer-based models demonstrated excellent performance, particularly for highly varying temporal sequences, yielding the lowest RMSE value of 0.25 at South Habshan (rural industrial area). In contrast, the LSTM model performed best at Ruwais (suburban industrial) station, producing an RMSE of 0.47 and was efficient for sequences with reduced variation. However, a notable limitation across all these studies is that their analyses relied exclusively on data from 2019–2020, a period heavily influenced by COVID-19 lockdowns, characterized by reduced mobility and atypical emission patterns, which may have altered pollutant behavior, therefore, limits the generalizability of their findings. A summary of significant related work is presented in Table 1.

Despite the popularity of forecasting using ML and DL models, several shortcomings remain, including inefficient model structures, inadequate datasets in terms of coverage period and sampling frequency, limited number of observation sites, and constrained forecasting horizons. Such limitations invariably affect the model scalability and generalizability. This study offers a detailed performance comparison of various ML and DL models, including Decision Trees, Random Forest, SVR, CNN, LSTM, Prophet, and transformer architectures, for predicting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} levels in Abu Dhabi, UAE. A rich and unique dataset comprising five years of hourly observations from seven ground monitoring stations across different regions of Abu Dhabi, is used to produce predictions over multiple time horizons. This study also evaluates the impact of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} coexistence on model performance. The rest of this paper is organized as follows. The Methodology section outlines the dataset, data pre-processing steps, hyperparameter selections, models, and evaluation metrics. Detailed comparisons of model performance are presented in the Result and Analysis section, followed by the Discussion and Future Work section. Summary of models applied for air pollutants forecasting across various datasets.

Methodology

Study area and dataset

The hourly data used in our analysis is provided by the Environment Agency – Abu Dhabi (EAD) and consist of air quality measurements collected from a network of seven permanent ground-level monitoring stations. As shown in Fig. 1, the stations are distributed across different regions of the city (Emirate) of Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates (UAE), and were selected to reflect the city diverse environmental conditions, including deserts, suburbs, cities, and suburb–industrial areas. The details of all monitoring stations are presented in Table 2. Baniyas station (Station 1) is in a desert-oriented area that includes urban developments and schools, with emissions from natural and anthropogenic sources. Al Ain Islamic Institute (Station 2) lies within suburban landscapes comprising residential areas, gardens, and greenery, where seasonal school traffic contributes to fluctuations in air quality. Bida Zayed (Station 3) is situated in a suburban residential zone and is subjected to emissions from cars, a local bus station and nearby driving schools. Gayathi School (Station 4) is marked by its suburban vegetations and garden plots, which serve as natural filters affecting the dispersion of pollutants. Bain Al Jessrain (Station 5) is adjacent to a main road and positioned within in a coastal area, where traffic emissions mix with coastal atmospheric pattern. The area surrounding Al Tawia Station (Station 6) is suburban, comprising schools, supermarkets, and urban greenery with relatively low pollution levels, although it is still subject to localized automobile emissions. For enhanced spatial representation, we included Ruwais Station (Station 7), located in Al Dhafra Region. Ruwais represents a suburban–industrial setting influenced by adjacent anthropogenic industrial activities and arid desert conditions, providing a unique perspective on pollution patterns shaped by human and natural factors. The dataset spans a continuous five-year period from January 2018 to December 2022, during which each station provided a total of 43,824 hourly measurements. Although the observation network measures several pollutants, such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{2.5}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PM}_{10}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {SO}_{2}$$\end{document}, CO, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}, this analysis focuses specifically on nitrogen dioxide (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document}) and ground-level ozone (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}) because of their importance in atmospheric chemistry and public health implications. This rich dataset facilitates both spatial and temporal analysis of pollutant variability and provides a strong platform for generating robust predictive air quality models. Data preprocessing

A series of preprocessing steps were applied to prepare the data for predictive modeling. To ensure robust model evaluation and assess prediction uncertainty, we employed 3-fold temporal cross-validation with an expanding window approach. For each fold, the dataset was chronologically split such that:Fold 1: First 25% for training, next 25% for testingFold 2: First 50% for training, next 25% for testingFold 3: First 75% for training, next 25% for testingThis expanding window strategy preserves temporal ordering as illustrated in Fig. 2, prevents information leakage, and simulates realistic operational forecasting scenarios where models are trained on increasingly larger historical datasets. A 10% validation subset from the training data was used for hyperparameter tuning in each fold. Model performance metrics (MAE, RMSE, sMAPE) are reported as mean ± standard deviation across the three folds, providing confidence intervals that quantify prediction uncertainty. This time-aware splitting strategy allows for independent hyperparameter tuning and model selection on the validation set, while maintaining an unbiased and realistic evaluation on the test set. Together, these steps provide a comprehensive and fair assessment of model generalization performance.Fig. 2Illustration of the 3-fold temporal cross-validation strategy. Each fold uses an expanding training window followed by a sequential test period, ensuring temporal order is preserved and providing robust uncertainty estimates.

Fold 1: First 25% for training, next 25% for testing

Fold 1: First 25% for training, next 25% for testing

Fold 2: First 50% for training, next 25% for testing

Fold 2: First 50% for training, next 25% for testing

Fold 3: First 75% for training, next 25% for testing

Fold 3: First 75% for training, next 25% for testing

Illustration of the 3-fold temporal cross-validation strategy. Each fold uses an expanding training window followed by a sequential test period, ensuring temporal order is preserved and providing robust uncertainty estimates.

Missing observations were handled using a leakage-aware imputation strategy designed specifically for time-series air quality data. Imputation parameters were derived exclusively from the training subset and then applied unchanged to the validation and testing subsets to prevent information leakage from future observations. Missing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} values were imputed using a calendar-based historical averaging approach. For each missing timestamp, the pollutant concentration was estimated as the mean of available observations recorded at the same day of the month, same month, and same hour of the day across other years, explicitly excluding data from the year 2020 due to the anomalous effects of COVID-19 lockdowns and related emission changes. This method preserves diurnal and seasonal pollution patterns while avoiding contamination from anomalous periods. The proportion of missing data varied across monitoring stations and pollutants but remained relatively low overall. As summarized in Table 4, missingness ranged from approximately 0.9% to 2.1% for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} and 1.1% to 4.1% for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} across the analyzed stations. Differences in missing data were primarily station dependent, reflecting sensor availability and maintenance schedules, rather than seasonally driven. No systematic seasonal missingness patterns were observed for either pollutant, reducing the likelihood of bias in model training and ensuring fairness in performance comparison across stations and forecast horizons.Table 4Missing data percentage per station for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_2$$\end{document}.StationID\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_3$$\end{document} Missing (%)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_2$$\end{document} Missing (%)11.93534.053222.11112.677031.17081.079540.91062.647451.76871.736861.79842.697672.03122.6314

Table 4Missing data percentage per station for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_2$$\end{document}.StationID\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_3$$\end{document} Missing (%)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_2$$\end{document} Missing (%)11.93534.053222.11112.677031.17081.079540.91062.647451.76871.736861.79842.697672.03122.6314

Missing data percentage per station for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_2$$\end{document}.

Unlike some common preprocessing methods, no measured pollutants values were removed or capped in consideration of outliers. Both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} are known to have sharp spikes due to episodic occurrences like traffic, industrial activities, or photochemical episodes during the summer. Keeping these values ensures the full variability of the dataset and enables the model to better capture extreme occurrences, which have important environmental and public health implications. A logarithmic transformation was applied to the pollutant concentration time series to stabilize variance and reduce skewness caused by extreme pollution events. Specifically, the transformation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x'=\log (x+1)$$\end{document} was applied to accommodate zero and near-zero values.The log transformation was fitted only on the training subset to avoid information leakage and was applied independently per pollutant and per monitoring station, preserving the station-specific distribution of concentrations. Prior to evaluation, model predictions were inverse transformed back to the original concentration scale by applying the exponential function and subtracting one. This ensured that all reported performance metrics reflect pollutant concentrations in their natural units. Finally, the time-series data were reformulated into a supervised learning framework using a rolling window approach. The input lag length was set equal to the forecasting horizon in all experiments. Specifically, a window of 1 hour was used for 1-hour-ahead prediction, 2 hours for 2-hour-ahead prediction, 24 hours for 1-day-ahead prediction, and 168 hours for 1-week-ahead prediction. Multi-step forecasting was implemented using a direct forecasting strategy, where models were trained to predict the pollutant concentration at the specified future time step directly from the corresponding input window. No recursive use of previous predictions was employed. This design avoids error accumulation associated with recursive forecasting and ensures a consistent and fair experimental protocol across different temporal horizons.

Inter-pollutant correlation analysis and feature augmentation

The correlation between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}was assessed at all monitoring stations prior to model construction. Pearson correlation coefficients were calculated for each station to quantitatively measure the strength and direction of the linear correlation between the two pollutants throughout the study period. The Pearson correlation coefficient is defined as32:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} r = \frac{\sum _{i=1}^{n} (X_i - \bar{X})(Y_i - \bar{Y})}{\sqrt{\sum _{i=1}^{n} (X_i - \bar{X})^2} \; \sqrt{\sum _{i=1}^{n} (Y_i - \bar{Y})^2}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_i, Y_i$$\end{document} are the individual data points, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{X}, \bar{Y}$$\end{document} are the means of X and Y, and n is the number of observations.

This analysis provided insights into how changes in one constituent relate to changes in another. Correlation results helped in designing a feature-augmentation experiment to evaluate the benefit of adding a single pollutant as an external variable for improving model predictive performance. Specifically, two different modelling setups were applied to each pollutant: (i) models based solely on the time-series data of the target pollutant, and (ii) models that included lagged values of the other pollutant as additional input variables. By using lagged cross-pollutant features, the models leverage temporally prior information from the correlated pollutant, ensuring that no future information is used when predicting the target pollutant at a given time step. This approach respects the causal temporal sequence and avoids data leakage. The lagged features also capture the delayed atmospheric and photochemical interactions that commonly occur between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} concentrations.

The results of such an enhancement were quantified for both short-term and long-term prediction intervals. The research sought to establish whether augmenting the feature sets with lagged information from correlated pollutants would substantially enhance prediction performance by comparing the forecasting accuracy of the single-pollutant and lagged dual-pollutant setups. Table 5 compiles the correlation coefficients for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} for all stations, providing a concise representation of the relationships between these pollutants and supporting the need for feature augmentation. The correlation coefficients highlight a negative relationship that consistently exist between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} across all stations, aligning with established photochemical principles whereby increasing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} concentrations are generally accompanied by lower \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} levels. Moderate to strong negative correlations are noted at most stations, with Stations 2, 5, and 6 being of primary interest, indicating that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} can be a desirable ancillary predictor for improving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} concentration forecast. Conversely, these negative correlations also imply that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} predictions may benefit from including \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} as an additional predictor, particularly in areas with stronger correlations. Stations with weaker correlations, such as Station 4, may feature more complex meteorological or emission conditions that mask primary correlations such as the interaction between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} Prediction models and hyperparameter optimization

A diverse selection of ML and DL models have been implemented to explore the variation in forecasting ability for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document}. These models represent a broad spectrum of approaches, ranging from interpretable tree-based regressors to state-of-the-art sequence learning architectures such as LSTMs and Transformers. Hyperparameter tuning was carefully conducted to ensure fair comparisons and to obtain robust performance results.

Tree-based models

Both ensemble-free and ensemble-based models were applied. The Decision Tree regressor provides a straightforward approach to learning nonlinear patterns through partitioning data into smaller subsets based on feature relevance. To avoid overfitting, maximum tree depth was limited to 10, representing a compromise between generalization and model complexity. The Random Forest regressor, a collection of several decorrelated trees, was also employed. It averages predictions from 100 trees, each trained using the squared-error as splitting criterion, to reduce variance and increase robustness. Random Forests are particularly efficient at learning complex interactions among predictors with better overfitting characteristics compared with a single decision tree.

Support Vector Regression

Support Vector Regression (SVR) was applied using both linear and Radial Basis Function (RBF) kernels. The linear SVR assumes a proportional relationship between the input variables and the pollutant levels, making it suitable for near-linear and short-term behavior. The RBF kernel, however, maps the input variables into a higher-dimensional space, where the model harness nonlinear associations and higher-order variations in pollutants levels resulting from meteorological conditions and anthropogenic activities.

Deep learning models

Both convolutional and recurrent architectures were created for deep learning models. The 1D-CNN constructed from a single convolutional layer (32 filters, kernel size = 2, ReLU activation), a flattening layer, and two dense layers. This structure is designed to cultivate local temporal patterns covering adjacent hours. The LSTM network consisted of 50 recurrent units utilizing ReLU activation, which facilitated the model’s ability to learn long-term temporal dependencies inherent in pollutant time series data. By maintaining contextual information over prolonged sequences, the LSTM architecture can effectively recognize both short-term variations and longer-term seasonal patterns. Both CNN and LSTM models were trained employing the Adam optimizer, with Mean Squared Error (MSE) designated as the loss function.

Transformer-based model

To capture global temporal relations, a dedicated Transformer encoder was employed33. In practice, the attention mechanism plays a key role in this design. The attention function is computed over a collection of queries represented in a matrix form Q, with the corresponding keys and values stored in matrices K and V33. Outputs come out as:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Attention}(Q, K, V) = \text {softmax}\left( \frac{QK^{T}}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right) V \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_k$$\end{document} denotes the dimensionality of the key vectors, used to scale the dot product and prevent excessively large values. The softmax function converts the resulting scores into a probability distribution and is defined as:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {softmax}(x_i) = \frac{e^{x_i}}{\sum _{j=1}^{n} e^{x_j}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}This formulation enables the model to adjust temporal dependencies efficiently. Each input sequence was first passed through an embedding layer (256 feature size), then positional encoding was applied to preserve sequential information. The encoder comprised six layers, each of which consisted of multi-head self-attention (4 heads), feed-forward sub-layers, layer normalization, and dropout of 0.1. For prediction, the encoder output corresponding to the most recent time step was extracted and passed through two fully connected layers separated by a ReLU activation. The final linear layer projected this representation onto the forecast horizon (1, 2, 24 or 168 hours ahead). Training was conducted over 50 epochs using the AdamW optimizer with MSE loss, and model selection was based on validation sMAPE. Figure 3 shows the architecture of the transformer model.Fig. 3Architecture of the transformer model, illustrating its core components.

Architecture of the transformer model, illustrating its core components.

Facebook prophet model

The Facebook Prophet model accounts for trends, seasons, and holiday effects. The additive element of Prophet is defined as34:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} y(t) = g(t) + s(t) + h(t) + \epsilon _t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where g(t) represents the trend component, s(t) models seasonality, h(t) models holiday effects, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _t$$\end{document} represents the error term. The Prophet model is notably adept at capturing seasonal and cyclical variations that affect air quality.

Hyperparameter tuning

Separate hyperparameters optimization was applied to ML and DL models. Grid search was applied to Decision Tree, Random Forest, SVR, and Prophet, where all possible combinations of parameters in specified ranges are systematically tested. For highly computationally expensive models such as CNN, LSTM, and Transformer, random search was used as it samples parameter combinations at random, revealing competing configurations at lower training cost. Hyperparameter tuning for different models.

Model evaluation

The predictive performance was evaluated using four metrics: Root Mean Square Error (RMSE), Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and symmetric Mean Absolute Percentage Error (sMAPE). Because large errors contribute disproportionately more than smaller errors, RMSE is particularly sensitive to large sudden changes or outliers in pollutant levels35.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {RMSE} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n} \sum _{i=1}^{n} (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}MAE presents the sum of the absolute differences between observed and predicted values, thus providing a transparent overview of overall accuracy35.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {MAE} = \frac{1}{n} \sum _{i=1}^{n} |y_i - \hat{y}_i|. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Finally, sMAPE expresses errors as a symmetric percentage, bounding the metric between 0% and 200% and avoiding the instability of sMAPE when observed values are close to zero35.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {sMAPE} = \frac{100}{n} \sum _{i=1}^{n} \frac{|y_i - \hat{y}_i|}{(|y_i| + |\hat{y}_i|) / 2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Collectively, these three metrics offer a comprehensive assessment of model performance, capturing error magnitude, scale independence, sensitivity to extreme values, and directional trends in predictions. All metrics are computed independently for each fold and reported as mean ± standard deviation, providing statistical confidence in model comparisons. This approach addresses potential variability due to temporal dynamics and ensures our conclusions are not dependent on a single train-test split.

Results and analysis

This section evaluates the predictive performance of the proposed machine learning and deep learning models across seven air quality monitoring stations in Abu Dhabi. Results are summarized using four tables: (i) baseline \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} forecasting, (ii) baseline \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} forecasting, (iii) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} forecasting augmented with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} as a correlated feature, and (iv) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} forecasting augmented with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} as a correlated feature. Each table reports averaged MAE, RMSE, and sMAPE across the seven stations, with standard deviations reflecting variability across temporal folds. Metrics are reported for short-term (1–2 h), medium-term (1 day), and long-term (1 week) horizons. To improve readability, the discussion focuses on key comparative trends, while detailed numerical values are referenced directly from Tables 7, 8, 9, 10. Representative prediction plots are provided in Figs. 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9 to illustrate model behavior across horizons and stations.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} forecasting results

As summarized in Table 7, the Transformer model consistently achieved the lowest average error values across all forecasting horizons, with narrow standard deviations indicating stable and generalizable performance. At the 1-hour horizon, the Transformer attained a mean MAE of 5.41, RMSE of 8.69, and sMAPE of 0.260, outperforming all other models. CNN ranked second for short-term prediction, producing competitive results at 1 and 2 hours. The SVR and LSTM models showed acceptable short-term performance but exhibited greater variability, particularly at longer horizons. A representative 1-hour forecast at Station 2 (Fig. 4) demonstrates the Transformer’s superior ability to follow rapid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} fluctuations. At the medium-term horizon (1 day), performance differences became more pronounced. Random Forest and SVR maintained relatively stable accuracy, while the Transformer continued to lead across all metrics with a mean MAE of 6.41 and sMAPE of 0.309. For long-term forecasting (1 week), the Transformer remained the best-performing model with a mean MAE of 6.78 and sMAPE of 0.329. Prophet emerged as the second-best performer at this horizon, reflecting its capacity to capture trend and seasonal components, despite being unavailable for sub-daily forecasting. In contrast, tree-based models and conventional neural networks (CNN, LSTM) suffered from significant error accumulation at the 1-week horizon, with Decision Tree reaching a mean sMAPE of 0.710. Figure 5 illustrates this divergence at Station 5. Overall, the Transformer provides the most robust and accurate performance across all time scales, while CNN is competitive for short-term prediction and Prophet performs well at longer horizons.Fig. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_2$$\end{document} Forecasting results (averaged across all stations).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} forecasting results

Table 8 reports the averaged \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} forecasting results. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} errors are considerably larger in absolute magnitude than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document}, reflecting the higher concentration range and variability of ozone. However, relative errors (sMAPE) are broadly comparable between the two pollutants. For short-term predictions (1–2 h), CNN consistently outperformed tree-based models, while the Transformer achieved the best or near-best performance in terms of sMAPE. Figure 6 (Station 3) highlights the clear advantage of CNN and Transformer models over decision trees and random forests. At the 1-day horizon, the Transformer clearly dominated, achieving a mean MAE of 11.19 and sMAPE of 0.289, while CNN and LSTM models exhibited increasing cumulative errors. Prophet showed limited capability at daily scales, yielding higher MAE and RMSE values. For weekly forecasts, all models experienced increased errors. However, the Transformer maintained the lowest overall error metrics with a mean MAE of 12.23 and sMAPE of 0.297, followed by Prophet. A notable observation is that SVR (RBF) degraded substantially at longer horizons, becoming one of the weakest models at 1-week despite its competitive short-term performance, suggesting poor generalization beyond short temporal windows. Impact of correlation-based feature augmentation

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} forecasting with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} correlation

Table 9 summarizes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} forecasting performance after incorporating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} as a correlated predictor. Comparing Tables 7 and 9, a consistent slight reduction in error metrics is observed across most models and horizons when correlation information is included. The Transformer remained the top-performing model and exhibited the most notable gains: at the 2-hours horizon, mean MAE decreased from 5.82 to 5.72, with more visible improvements at medium- and long-term horizons, where RMSE and sMAPE values were slightly reduced. CNN models also exhibited modest improvements at short horizons. At the 1-week horizon, Prophet similarly benefited from correlation inclusion, with slight sMAPE reductions observed across stations. Tree-based models showed mixed but generally modest improvements. Figure 8 illustrates the improved 1-day forecast behavior under the augmented model. Overall, the addition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} as a correlated feature yields consistent, if incremental, improvements in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} forecasting accuracy, with the Transformer benefiting the most across all horizons.Fig. 8Daily \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} forecasting at Station 2: Transformer model without \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} correlation (top) versus Transformer model with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} correlation (bottom), showing performance improvement with correlated input.Table 9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} Table 10 summarizes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} forecasting performance when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {NO}_{2}$$\end{document} is included as an additional input. Relative to baseline results (Table 8), most models exhibited a slight decrease in error metrics, particularly at medium- and long-term horizons. The Transformer and CNN showed the clearest improvements: for example, the Transformer’s mean sMAPE at the 1-day horizon decreased from 0.289 to 0.281, and RMSE showed a corresponding reduction. Figure 9 demonstrates the improved temporal alignment between predicted and observed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}_{3}$$\end{document} values. Short-term gains were more modest but consistently present for SVR and CNN models. Tree-based models showed smaller, more variable responses to the augmentation. Prophet displayed incremental improvements for weekly forecasts.
